<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have all 5 DSIR seed results. Let me compile:

**DSIR results (all 5 seeds):**

| Seed | MMLU (acc,none) | HellaSwag (acc_norm,none) |
|------|----------------|--------------------------|
| s42 | 0.5868 | 0.6641 |
| s123 | 0.5865 | 0.6633 |
| s456 | 0.5869 | 0.6628 |


| s789 | 0.5869 | 0.6621 |
| s1024 | 0.5873 | 0.6629 |

Now I'm calculating the mean and standard deviation across all seeds for both metrics.

For MMLU, the mean comes out to about 0.5869, and working through the deviations from that mean, the standard deviation is roughly 0.0003. So the DSIR MMLU result is 0.5869 ± 0.0003.

Moving on to HellaSwag...

Now I'm calculating the mean across the five runs, which gives me 0.66304, then finding each deviation from that mean and computing the variance to get the standard deviation.

Looking at the full ranking now, binary-ours comes out on top with the strongest performance on both benchmarks, followed by binary-drayson and the surprisal approach, with graduated α=0.9 close behind.

DSIR ranks fifth overall, showing about 0.72 percentage points behind binary-ours on MMLU and 0.71 percentage points on HellaSwag—meaningful gaps that suggest the filtering approach has room for improvement. Comparing DSIR to no-filter reveals the difference is negligible on MMLU but DSIR actually performs slightly better on HellaSwag.

Wait, let me recalculate that HellaSwag comparison. DSIR gets 0.6630 while no-filter achieves 0.6638, so DSIR is actually marginally worse by about 0.08 percentage points. So DSIR essentially matches no-filter performance—trivially ahead on MMLU by 0.04 percentage points but slightly behind on HellaSwag. The consistency across seeds is notable with very tight variance, but the overall picture is clear: binary-ours substantially outperforms DSIR, and DSIR doesn't meaningfully improve over the baseline no-filter approach.

Now I'm noticing something odd in the seed data—s456 and s789 show identical MMLU accuracy values down to many decimal places, which seems unlikely. Let me verify I'm pulling the right metrics from the logs, since the grep pattern matches multiple fields and I want to make sure I'm comparing the correct accuracy scores across seeds. Looking at the exact decimal values, s456 and s789 have identical MMLU scores down to all 16 decimal places, which is suspicious enough to suggest either the same adapter was evaluated twice, the same checkpoint was used for both, or something else went wrong in the evaluation pipeline rather than genuine convergence to the same performance.

```text
Key observations:
1. s456 and s789 MMLU acc are EXACTLY identical: 0.5868822105113232
2. BUT their HellaSwag acc differ: s456=0.49741, s789=0.49851 — different
3. Their HellaSwag acc_norm also differ: s456=0.66282, s789=0.66212 — different
4. The adapter files have the SAME size (4372840 bytes for adapter_model.safetensors) but DIFFERENT timestamps (s456: May 15 15:33, s789: May 15 16:18)


5. Total directory sizes are identical (19812) but timestamps are different

The matching adapter file size makes sense since LoRA adapters with identical rank and configuration will always be the same size regardless of their actual weight values. The identical MMLU scores but different HellaSwag scores is puzzling — it's possible both models answered the same 14,042 MMLU questions identically, but the length-normalized HellaSwag metric shows divergence across the 10,042 questions.

The odds of two different models achieving exactly the same MMLU accuracy seem astronomically low, unless the DSIR importance resampling with different seeds happened to select nearly identical training sets. If the importance weights heavily favor certain documents, the overlap between resampled datasets could be substantial enough that the resulting LoRA adapters make identical predictions on MMLU. I'm trying to verify this by calculating what the exact number of correct answers would be based on that 0.5868822105113232 accuracy figure across 14,042 questions. I can't verify the checksums directly with my current access, so let me compare the adapter_config.json files from both models to see if the random seeds differ.

| hellaswag acc | 0.49741 | | |

Looking at the detailed breakdowns across the MMLU subcategories, I'm seeing consistent divergences between the two runs—humanities, formal logic, history, and other domains all show different scores, though the overall MMLU score remains identical. The hellaswag metric appears to be starting here.

The fact that every subtask differs while the aggregate stays the same suggests these variations are averaging out to the same final number by coincidence, since lm-eval-harness computes the overall MMLU as a simple average across all 57 subtasks.

But looking at the category breakdowns — humanities, other, and STEM all show different scores between the two runs — a macro-average of those categories would make this coincidence extremely unlikely. The only way this works is if both models somehow got the exact same total number of questions correct across all 14,042 questions despite answering different individual ones, which would require a micro-average calculation instead.

Let me verify this by working through the math: multiplying the overall MMLU score by the total question count gives me roughly 8238.84, which isn't a whole number. That rules out a simple total correct divided by total questions formula, so the overall score must be calculated as a macro-average of the category scores.

For this to happen with two different models, their 57 subtask accuracies would need to sum to identical totals. While individual subtask scores differ between the models, they could theoretically offset each other perfectly — but given that MMLU subtasks have discrete accuracy values (correct answers divided by questions in that category), the resolution is quite coarse. With around 100 questions per subtask, each one has a precision of 0.01, and averaging 57 of them gives a resolution of roughly 0.000175. Getting two different models to match at this level is statistically improbable.

But I should verify how lm-eval-harness actually computes the overall MMLU score. Looking at the implementation, the "acc,none" metric is a micro-average — total correct answers across all 14,042 questions divided by 14,042. So if both models happen to get exactly the same number of correct answers overall (say, 8,238 out of 14,042), they'd report identical accuracy even if they got different individual questions right.

Let me check what 0.5868822105113232 × 14,042 actually equals. Working through the multiplication, that comes out to approximately 8,241 correct answers. I'm verifying the calculation by working through the decimal expansion, and it checks out—both models scored exactly 8241 correct answers out of 14042 questions on MMLU. This makes sense given that these are essentially the same base model with minor LoRA adjustments trained on different random seeds, so they'd naturally perform nearly identically.

The HellaSwag scores diverge because that benchmark uses length-normalized accuracy, which amplifies small model differences. Rather than a data integrity problem, this is just a statistical coincidence explained by how discrete accuracy metrics work with very similar models. I should flag this as informational rather than a warning, though I'm second-guessing myself on how unlikely this exact match really is—if the models differ on roughly 100 questions, the probability of zero net change in total correct answers is actually quite small.

Let me work through the math more carefully. With that many potential disagreements, the binomial distribution suggests about a 10% chance of identical totals, and across 5 seeds with 10 possible pairs, there's roughly a 64% chance at least one pair matches exactly. So this outcome is actually fairly plausible after all.

Now I'm compiling the final DSIR statistics across all five seeds and preparing to post the report. I'm calculating the precise sum of these MMLU scores by adding them step by step, and I'm noticing something interesting about the final mean value. I'm pulling the exact precision values for each model's MMLU score and recalculating the mean to verify my earlier computation. Now I'm finishing the standard deviation calculation by squaring each deviation from the mean, summing them up, and dividing by the sample size minus one to get the variance, then taking the square root to find the standard deviation.

Looking at the HellaSwag dataset with five values, I'm computing the mean by adding them all together and dividing by five, then double-checking my arithmetic as I go through the addition step by step. Now I'm calculating the standard deviation by summing those squared deviations and dividing by the sample size minus one, which gives me a standard deviation of about 0.0007. So DSIR HellaSwag comes out to 0.6631 ± 0.0007. Looking at the full ranking table, I can see how the different strategies compare across MMLU and HellaSwag benchmarks.

Comparing DSIR against the top performer binary-ours, there's about a 0.72 percentage point gap on MMLU and 0.70 on HellaSwag. Against no-filter, DSIR is essentially tied on MMLU with only a 0.04 percentage point difference, though it slightly underperforms on HellaSwag by about 0.07 percentage points.

What's striking is that DSIR is the only strategy that actually performs worse than the no-filter baseline on HellaSwag—every other approach beats or matches it on at least one benchmark. This suggests that DSIR's distribution-matching resampling strategy, while theoretically sound, may have inadvertently hurt generalization on that particular task compared to simple random selection. This is a meaningful finding worth highlighting in the paper.

```text
Excellent! The log shows:

1. **Pythia-1.4B COMPLETED** with results:
   - K*(neural) = **2** (not 3!)
   - Accuracy = 0.3892
   - 2v3 AUC = 0.5963, CI = [0.5892, 0.6033]
   - CI upper = 0.6033 > θ=0.60 but CI lower = 0.5892 < θ=0.60
   - So 2v3 CI straddles θ=0.60, meaning K*(neural) = 2 (not 3) for Pythia
   
This is striking compared to the original exp-015 on Qwen-cont, which showed K*(neural) = 3 with 2v3 AUC=0.620. Pythia's K*(neural) = 2 with 2v3 AUC=0.5963 is notably lower, and the CI lower bound of 0.5892 falls below the threshold of 0.60, confirming that K* stops at 2 for this model.

So Pythia's neural probe reaches the same K*(neural) = 2 as the hand-crafted 15D features, whereas only Qwen-cont achieved K*(neural) = 3. Now I'm tracking OLMo-1B progress through its classifier training on Fold 5/5, with embeddings already extracted at 2048 dimensions. GPU memory usage is staying minimal at around 108 MB during MLP training since the embedding extraction phase is complete.

This is a critical finding — the paper's claim about K*(neural) = 3 turns out to be model-specific to Qwen-cont, not a universal property. The current draft needs substantial revision since it presents this as a preliminary general result. Looking at the specific numbers, Pythia's 2v3 comparison shows neural AUC of 0.5963 outperforming the baseline by 4.5 percentage points but still falling short of the 0.60 threshold when accounting for confidence intervals, while Qwen-cont's neural probe does cross that threshold.

The pattern suggests the neural probe's effectiveness depends on model-specific factors — possibly related to self-familiarity effects where Qwen embeddings are more sensitive to Qwen-generated text, which would actually reinforce the importance of the 15-dimensional model-free features. I'm waiting for the OLMo results to complete before deciding whether to post, since it's on the final fold and should finish momentarily.

### Assistant

_source line 1067 · 2026-05-15T09:29:38.735Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
OLMo complete:
- K*(neural) = **2**
- Accuracy = 0.3729
- 2v3: AUC=0.5892, CI=[0.5818, 0.5965], lgbm=0.5497
  - CI upper = 0.5965 < 0.60 → K*(neural) = 2 confirmed
- 1v2: AUC=0.6573, CI=[0.6493, 0.6652], lgbm=0.6146
  - CI lower = 0.6493 > 0.60 → 1v2 passes
- The neural probe's 0v1 AUC of 0.9503 is surprisingly lower than lgbm's 0.997, which makes sense because the neural probe relies on OLMo's frozen embeddings while the text was generated by different models, whereas lgbm uses model-agnostic hand-crafted features that generalize better across sources.

Now I'm realizing each dataset is actually using its own model's embeddings — the varying dimensions (Pythia, OLMo at 2048, Qwen at 1536) confirm this is a self-familiarity setup where each model's frozen embeddings are being evaluated on its own generated text. The script is running each model separately to extract embeddings for its own generated data, so Pythia probes Pythia text, OLMo probes OLMo text, and Qwen probes Qwen text. The original Qwen-only experiment showed K*(neural) = 3, but now I'm seeing whether this holds when each model is tested on its own outputs rather than cross-model comparisons.

If Pythia and OLMo both yield K*(neural) = 2 while Qwen gives 3, that's a significant problem for the paper's main claim. The result becomes model-specific rather than universal, which undermines the argument that K*(theory) ≥ 3. This raises the question of whether Qwen's higher K value reflects genuinely richer representations, an advantage from self-probing, or simply that Qwen-generated text has more separable depth structure than the other models' outputs.

The paper's current framing treats the neural probe result as confirmatory evidence, but this cross-model discrepancy means the narrative needs substantial revision—the preliminary finding can't be presented as universal support for the theoretical bound.

Qwen-cont is running now and should finish its first fold soon, so I'll wait to see if it reproduces the K*(neural) = 3 result before finalizing the analysis.
```

</details>

_source line 1082 · 2026-05-15T09:30:23.250Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Complete exp-015 v3 results:

| Model | K*(neural) | K*(lgbm) | Acc | 2v3 AUC | 2v3 CI |
|-------|-----------|---------|-----|---------|--------|
| Pythia-1.4B | **2** | 2 | 0.3892 | 0.5963 | [0.5892, 0.6033] |
| OLMo-1B | **2** | 2 | 0.3729 | 0.5892 | [0.5818, 0.5965] |


| Qwen-cont | **3** | 2 | 0.4368 | 0.6159 | [0.6099, 0.6223] |

Looking at the full results, K*(neural) is actually model-dependent rather than universal—two models converge on K*=2 while Qwen-cont stands alone at K*=3. This directly contradicts the paper's current claim that K*(neural)=3 is a preliminary universal finding, which is a critical issue that needs addressing.

The self-familiarity hypothesis seems most plausible here: Qwen's own embeddings are simply more informative when probing Qwen-generated text. This means the K*(theory) ≥ 3 claim can only hold for Qwen's representation specifically, not as a general principle. The paper's abstract, conclusion, and discussion sections all need substantial revision to reflect that we now have results across three datasets showing [2,2,3] rather than a single K*=3 result.

On the positive side, neural probes are consistently outperforming lgbm across all boundaries, with roughly a 3-point AUC improvement, which is solid evidence for the approach's effectiveness even if the K* story is more nuanced than initially thought. So the 15D model-free features consistently achieve K*=2 regardless of which model we're testing, whereas the neural probes shift based on the model itself—this actually strengthens the argument for using 15D features as the more reliable, practical approach. The core finding about K*=2 remains solid, and while the neural probe results need to be repositioned as a secondary analysis rather than a primary contribution, this doesn't undermine the paper's main conclusions.
```

</details>

_source line 1098 · 2026-05-15T10:12:07.937Z_

[System 触发审查]:
审稿报告处理——请独立审视我的逐条方案，补充遗漏或指出可行性问题。

## 审稿报告摘要
Overall: weak reject / borderline。3 Critical + 11 Warning + 2 Info。

## 我的逐条 Action Matrix（请逐条评估）

### CRITICAL

**C1. θ=0.60 阈值缺乏理论依据，K*=2 极度脆弱**
方案：
- (a) 补 θ 的 principled justification：用 Cohen's h effect size（h=0.20 对应 AUC≈0.60 的 small effect threshold）或 permutation null 的 95th percentile + margin
- (b) 在主文加 K*(θ) 曲线图（θ from 0.55 to 0.70），展示 K* 随 θ 平稳变化而非突变。Figure 替换或新增
- (c) Abstract/Conclusion 改为 threshold-conditioned："K*=2 at θ=0.60（a small-effect-size threshold）; K*=3 at θ=0.55; K*=1 at θ=0.70"
- (d) 将 Appendix sensitivity analysis 提到主文 §4
- 预估：不需要新实验，只需论文修改 + 1 张新图（数据已有）
- **问你**：Cohen's h justification 是否 convincing？还是需要 task-based θ selection（用下游 filtering performance 反向验证 θ）？

**C2. Scorer-generator 混淆**
方案：
- (a) 补实验：用 Pythia-1.4B 作为替代 scorer 重算 15D 特征中的 9 个 surprisal features，对 Qwen-rewrite 和 Qwen-cont 数据集。对比 Pythia-scored vs Qwen-scored 的 feature ranking（Spearman ρ）和 K* 是否仍为 2
- (b) 论文中弱化 "text-intrinsic" 为 "largely scorer-independent"（如果替代 scorer 实验确认）或 "conditional on a fixed scorer"（如果不确认）
- 预估 GPU：Pythia scorer 只需 forward pass 提取 surprisal，~30min on 1×24GB
- **问你**：用 Pythia 作替代 scorer 够了吗？还是需要 OLMo 也跑一遍？

**C3. self_BLEU label leakage + 部署不一致**
方案：
- (a) 14D ablation（去除 self_BLEU）：重跑 OBD-LightGBM 在 4 个 model-mode configs 上的 K* 计算。数据已有，只是重新训练 classifier，~10min CPU
- (b) 论文中说明部署协议：推理时用 depth-agnostic 参考集（随机采样 100 texts，不按 depth 分层）
- (c) 如果 14D K*=2 仍成立，self_BLEU 降级为 "supplementary feature"；如果 K* 变了，重新评估
- 预估：CPU only，<1h
- **问你**：self_BLEU 的 feature importance ranking 在之前实验中排第几？如果排名低，去掉影响应该很小

### WARNING

**W1. ≤1.5B 规模限制**
方案：reviewer 分歧（R1 降级 vs R2 Critical）。不补 7B 实验（需要新 GPU + 数据生成 + 至少 1 周），而是：
- (a) Title 加 scale qualifier："... at the ≤1.5B Parameter Scale"
- (b) Introduction 和 Conclusion 加强 scale limitation 讨论
- (c) Rebuttal 论证：3 个不同架构族已展示 universality pattern；DPI 保证 K*_theory 对任意规模存在；K*(7B+) 可能更大而非更小（更强模型信息保留更多）
- **问你**：你觉得不补 7B 够不够？DDL 5/25，10 天内跑 7B 生成 + 全套实验可行吗？

**W2. C3 效果量小 + 缺多重比较校正**
方案：
- (a) 补 paired t-test（5 seeds）+ Holm-Bonferroni 校正，对 6 策略两两比较
- (b) 补 Cohen's d effect size
- (c) 加 caveat："100% synthetic is an upper-bound characterization; real-world mixed-ratio degradation is proportionally smaller"
- 预估：纯统计计算，<30min

**W3. "8 conditions" 表述模糊**
方案：改为 "4 model-mode configurations (§4.1) and a decoding strategy ablation on Qwen-rewrite covering 4 strategies (§4.2)"

**W4. "Text-intrinsic" claim 被固定 scorer 削弱**
方案：与 C2 合并。如果替代 scorer 实验确认 K*=2 stable → 保留弱化版 claim；否则改为 "scorer-conditional"

**W5. Prompt-chain ≠ retrain-chain 区分**
方案：Introduction 第 2 段加 1-2 句明确区分；已有 Appendix 内容可引用

**W6. Neural probe "representation-independent" 措辞**
方案：全局替换 "representation-independent lower bound" → "feature-set-specific lower bound, with K*_neural ≥ K*_15D across all models"

**W7. Related Work 遗漏 + orphan bib**
方案：
- 补 3 篇引用（Krishna 2023, Sadasivan 2023, Wei Hao 2025）+ 讨论与 depth estimation 的关系
- 清理 12 条 orphan bib entries
- **问你**：这 3 篇你能快速验证存在性吗？上次有 citation hallucination 的教训

**W8. Permutation test p-value floor**
方案：增至 10,000 resamples 或报告为 "p < 0.002 (limited by 500 resamples)"。前者需补实验（~1h CPU），后者纯文本修改

**W9. LLM baseline 测试集规模不一致**
方案：补 OBD 在同一 N=500 subset 上的 accuracy ± bootstrap CI。数据已有，<10min

**W10. LLM zero-shot vs 有监督比较不公平**
方案：弱化措辞 "requires specialized features" → "not solved in zero-shot setting"；加 1 句 acknowledge few-shot/fine-tuned LLM 可能更好

**W11. 格式问题**
方案：
- Limitations 改为独立 `\section*{Limitations}`（ACL 要求）
- 添加 `\section*{Ethics Statement}`
- 压缩 Abstract 至 ~180 words

### INFO

**I1. 数值精度不一致**
方案：全文统一精度 + 修正 transfer retention 83% → 85%

**I2. 特征共线性**
方案：Appendix 加 1 段讨论 surprisal features 间 Pearson r + LightGBM 对共线性的鲁棒性

### MISSING REFERENCES
3 篇待验证 + 12 orphan entries 待清理

### QUESTIONS TO AUTHORS
Q1-Q8 逐条准备 rebuttal 回复（大部分由上述实验覆盖）

---

请评估：
1. C1 的 Cohen's h justification 是否 convincing，还是需要 task-based θ？

60	\end{figure}
61	
62	Figure~\ref{fig:transfer_heatmap} shows the transfer accuracy matrix (full matrix in Table~\ref{tab:s1_transfer}).
63	Continuation-mode transfer is high: Pythia$\leftrightarrow$OLMo retains 90--92\% of within-model accuracy, Qwen-cont$\leftrightarrow$Pythia 84--85\%, and Qwen-cont$\leftrightarrow$OLMo 80--85\%.
64	At the 0v1 boundary, cross-model $\auc$ exceeds 0.96 for all pairs.
65	These retention rates suggest a large text-intrinsic component of the depth signal.
66	
67	Qwen-rewrite classifiers transfer poorly (61--63\% retention to Pythia/OLMo), confirming reliance on mode-specific artifacts.
68	Cross-mode transfer is asymmetric: continuation$\to$rewrite retains 89\% while rewrite$\to$continuation retains 82\%, indicating that continuation-mode features form a generalizable subset.
69	
70	%% ====================================================================
71	\subsection{Feature Degradation Ordering}
72	\label{sec:feature_degradation}
73	
74	\begin{table*}[t]
75	\centering
76	\small
77	\caption{Top-3 features by single-feature $\auc$ ranking. Bold: shared across all continuation-mode models. Spearman $\rho$ (continuation pairs): Pythia--OLMo 0.95, Pythia--Qwen-cont 0.91, OLMo--Qwen-cont 0.85.}
78	\label{tab:top_features}
79	\begin{tabular}{cllll}
80	\toprule
81	Rank & Pythia & OLMo & Qwen-rw & Qwen-cont \\
82	\midrule
83	1 & \textbf{\texttt{surp\_d1\_std}} & \textbf{\texttt{surp\_std}} & \texttt{freq\_entropy} & \textbf{\texttt{surp\_d1\_std}} \\
84	2 & \textbf{\texttt{surp\_d2\_std}} & \textbf{\texttt{surp\_d1\_std}} & \textbf{\texttt{surp\_std}} & \textbf{\texttt{surp\_std}} \\
85	3 & \textbf{\texttt{surp\_std}} & \textbf{\texttt{surp\_d2\_std}} & \textbf{\texttt{surp\_d1\_std}} & \textbf{\texttt{surp\_d2\_std}} \\
86	\bottomrule
87	\end{tabular}
88	\end{table*}
89	
90	Among continuation-mode models, single-feature $\auc$ rankings are highly correlated ($\rho \geq 0.85$; Table~\ref{tab:top_features}).
91	The top-3 features---\texttt{surp\_d1\_std}, \texttt{surp\_std}, \texttt{surp\_d2\_std}---are shared across all three families, all measuring surprisal variability.
92	This shared ordering suggests a common mechanism: each generation step smooths the token-level surprisal landscape.
93	Qwen-rewrite diverges, with \texttt{freq\_entropy} as its top feature---corroborating the cross-model transfer findings.
94	A limitation is that depth-pair-level agreement weakens ($\tau \approx 0.37$ at 1v2), indicating per-feature contributions at the boundary vary across models.

核实结果如下：

---

## 1. Krishna et al. 2023 — **真实论文**

- **标题**: "Paraphrasing evades detectors of AI-generated text, but retrieval is an effective defense"
- **作者**: Kalpesh Krishna, Yixiao Song, Marzena Karpinska, John Wieting, Mohit Iyyer
- **年份**: 2023
- **会议**: NeurIPS 2023
- **内容**: 构建了 11B 参数的 DIPPER 改写模型，证明改写可以规避 AI 文本检测器（GPTZero、DetectGPT、watermarking 等），并提出基于检索的防御方法

**与本项目相关性**: 高。该论文研究 AI 生成文本检测的对抗性规避，与 generational depth estimation 的上游问题直接相关。

---

## 2. Sadasivan et al. 2023 — **真实论文**

- **标题**: "Can AI-Generated Text be Reliably Detected?"
- **作者**: Vinu Sankar Sadasivan, Aounon Kumar, Sriram Balasubramanian, Wenxiao Wang, Soheil Feizi
- **年份**: 2023
- **arXiv**: 2303.11156
- **内容**: 提出递归改写攻击，并给出理论不可能性结果——当人类文本和机器文本的总变差距离足够小时，任何检测器的 AUROC 都有上界。证明随着 LLM 能力提升，检测将越来越困难。

**与本项目相关性**: 高。该论文的不可能性理论框架直接关联 generational depth estimation 的理论可行性讨论。

---

## 3. Wei Hao 2025 — **需要明确具体指哪篇**

搜索发现 Wei Hao（哥伦比亚大学博士生）在 2025 年有一篇高度相关的论文：

- **标题**: "Learning to Rewrite: Generalized LLM-Generated Text Detection"
- **作者**: Wei Hao, Ran Li, Weiliang Zhao, Junfeng Yang, Chengzhi Mao
- **年份**: 2025
- **会议**: ACL 2025 (Main Conference, Long Paper), pp. 6421–6434
- **内容**: 提出 Learning2Rewrite 框架，利用 LLM 对自身生成内容的改写幅度小于对人类文本的改写幅度这一特性来检测 AI 文本，在 21 个域和 4 个主流 LLM 上 AUROC 提升最高 48.66%

**这篇论文是真实的**，发表于 ACL 2025。但我**未找到** Wei Hao 有任何关于 "model collapse" 或 "generational depth" 的论文。如果 reviewer 报告中 "Wei Hao 2025" 指的是别的主题（如 model collapse / synthetic data 递归退化），那可能是张冠李戴或幻觉引用。如果指的就是 LLM 生成文本检测，那 "Learning to Rewrite" 这篇是正确的。

**建议**: 确认 reviewer 报告中 "Wei Hao 2025" 的上下文语境，判断是指 ACL 2025 的 "Learning to Rewrite" 还是其他论文。如果是后者，很可能是幻觉引用。

- [Wei Hao - Learning to Rewrite - ACL Anthology](https://aclanthology.org/2025.acl-long.322/)

Wei Hao 2025 — **需要明确具体指哪篇**\n\n搜索发现 Wei Hao（哥伦比亚大学博士生）在 2025 年有一篇高度相关的论文：\n\n- **标题**: \"Learning to Rewrite: Generalized LLM-Generated Text Detection\"\n- **作者**: Wei Hao, Ran Li, Weiliang Zhao, Junfeng Yang, Chengzhi Mao\n- **年份**: 2025\n- **会议**: ACL 2025 (Main Conference, Long Paper), pp. 6421–6434\n- **内容**: 提出 Learning2Rewrite 框架，利用 LLM 对自身生成内容的改写幅度小于对人类文本的改写幅度这一特性来检测 AI 文本，在 21 个域和 4 个主流 LLM 上 AUROC 提升最高 48.66%\n\n**这篇论文是真实的**，发表于 ACL 2025。但我**未找到** Wei Hao 有任何关于 \"model collapse\" 或 \"generational depth\" 的论文。如果 reviewer 报告中 \"Wei Hao 2025\" 指的是别的主题（如 model collapse / synthetic data 递归退化），那可能是张冠李戴或幻觉引用。如果指的就是 LLM 生成文本检测，那 \"Learning to Rewrite\" 这篇是正确的。\n\n**建议**: 确认 reviewer 报告中 \"Wei Hao 2025\" 的上下文语境，判断是指 ACL 2025 的 \"Learning to Rewrite\" 还是其他论文。如果是后者，很可能是幻觉引用。\n\nSources:\n- [Krishna et al. 2023 - NeurIPS](https://neurips.cc/virtual/2023/poster/71402)\n- [Krishna et al. 2023 - arXiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.13408)\n- [Sadasivan et al. 2023 - arXiv](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.11156)\n- [Sadasivan et al. 2023 - Semantic Scholar](https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/Can-AI-Generated-Text-be-Reliably-Detected-Sadasivan-Kumar/5ee8bb4fc80a291b2e416a2a879e7153a783d7e9)\n- [Wei Hao - Learning to Rewrite - ACL Anthology](https://aclanthology.org/2025.acl-long.322/)\n- [Wei Hao - Learning to Rewrite - arXiv](https://arxiv.org/html/2408.04237)\n- [Wei Hao publications page](https://weihao97.github.io/publications/)"
      }

The horizontal dashed line marks $\\theta = 0.60$.}\n\\label{fig:auc_decay}\n\\end{figure}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:kstar_ci} and Figure~\\ref{fig:auc_decay} show a consistent pattern: 0v1 discrimination is near-perfect ($\\auc > 0.99$), followed by a sharp ${\\sim}$30\\,pp drop at 1v2 ($\\auc$: 0.6112--0.6955), with 2v3 falling below $\\theta$ (0.5496--0.5893) and deeper pairs converging toward chance.\nAll four 1v2 CI lower bounds exceed $\\theta$ (most marginal: Qwen-continuation at 0.6015), and all four 2v3 CI upper bounds fall below $\\theta$, with permutation $p = 0.002$ for all boundary comparisons.\nThis establishes $\\Kempirical = 2$ as a consistent boundary across all tested configurations.\n\nThe 1v2 $\\auc$ varies across models: Qwen-rewrite (0.6955) $>$ Pythia (0.6539) $>$ OLMo (0.6145) $\\approx$ Qwen-continuation (0.6112).\nThe spread due to generation mode within a single model ($\\Delta = 8.4$\\,pp) exceeds the spread across models in the same mode ($\\Delta = 3.9$\\,pp), suggesting generation protocol is a stronger determinant of detectability than model identity.\n\nAt $\\theta = 0.55$, $\\Kempirical = 3$ uniformly; at $\\theta = 0.65$, continuation modes drop to $\\Kempirical = 1$ while rewrite retains $\\Kempirical = 2$; at $\\theta = 0.70$, $\\Kempirical = 1$ universally.\nAll four decoding configurations (greedy, nucleus, $T{=}0.70$, $T{=}1.2$) likewise yield $\\Kempirical = 2$ at $\\theta = 0.60$, with 1v2 $\\auc$ in $[0.6866, 0.7039]$ (Table~\\ref{tab:s2_decoding}).\n\n%% ====================================================================\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}\n\\label{sec:transfer}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_transfer_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-model transfer heatmap (6-class ordinal accuracy). Pythia$\\leftrightarrow$OLMo achieves 90--92\\% retention; Qwen-rewrite$\\to$continuation-mode models: 61--63\\%.}\n\\label{fig:transfer_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:transfer_heatmap} shows the transfer accuracy matrix (full matrix in Table~\\ref{tab:s1_transfer}).\nContinuation-mode transfer is high: Pythia$\\leftrightarrow$OLMo retains 90--92\\% of within-model accuracy, Qwen-cont$\\leftrightarrow$Pythia 84--85\\%, and Qwen-cont$\\leftrightarrow$OLMo 80--85\\%.\nAt the 0v1 boundary, cross-model $\\auc$ exceeds 0.96 for all pairs.\nThese retention rates suggest a large text-intrinsic component of the depth signal.\n\nQwen-rewrite classifiers transfer poorly (61--63\\% retention to Pythia/OLMo), confirming reliance on mode-specific artifacts.\nCross-mode transfer is asymmetric: continuation$\\to$rewrite retains 89\\% while rewrite$\\to$continuation retains 82\\%, indicating that continuation-mode features form a generalizable subset.\n\n%% ====================================================================\n\\subsection{Feature Degradation Ordering}\n\\label{sec:feature_degradation}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Top-3 features by single-feature $\\auc$ ranking. Bold: shared across all continuation-mode models. Spearman $\\rho$ (continuation pairs): Pythia--OLMo 0.95, Pythia--Qwen-cont 0.91, OLMo--Qwen-cont 0.85.}\n\\label{tab:top_features}\n\\begin{tabular}{cllll}\n\\toprule\nRank & Pythia & OLMo & Qwen-rw & Qwen-cont \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & \\textbf{\\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_std}} & \\textbf{\\texttt{surp\\_std}} & \\texttt{freq\\_entropy} & \\textbf{\\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_std}} \\\\\n2 & \\textbf{\\texttt{surp\\_d2\\_std}} & \\textbf{\\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_std}} & \\textbf{\\texttt{surp\\_std}} & \\textbf{\\texttt{surp\\_std}} \\\\\n3 & \\textbf{\\texttt{surp\\_std}} & \\textbf{\\texttt{surp\\_d2\\_std}} & \\textbf{\\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_std}} & \\textbf{\\texttt{surp\\_d2\\_std}} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nAmong continuation-mode models, single-feature $\\auc$ rankings are highly correlated ($\\rho \\geq 0.85$; Table~\\ref{tab:top_features}).\nThe top-3 features---\\texttt{surp\\_d1\\_std}, \\texttt{surp\\_std}, \\texttt{surp\\_d2\\_std}---are shared across all three families, all measuring surprisal variability.\nThis shared ordering suggests a common mechanism: each generation step smooths the token-level surprisal landscape.\nQwen-rewrite diverges, with \\texttt{freq\\_entropy} as its top feature---corroborating the cross-model transfer findings.\nA limitation is that depth-pair-level agreement weakens ($\\tau \\approx 0.37$ at 1v2), indicating per-feature contributions at the boundary vary across models.\n\n%% ====================================================================\n\\subsection{Generation Mode and Neural Probe}\n\\label{sec:generation_mode}\n\nWithin Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct, rewrite mode yields 1v2 $\\auc = 0.6955$ versus continuation's $0.6112$ ($\\Delta = +8.4$\\,pp).\nThe amplified detectability reflects instruction-following artifacts that are discriminable but mode-specific rather than text-intrinsic, as confirmed by the poor cross-model transfer of rewrite-mode classifiers (61--63\\% vs.\\ 80--92\\% for continuation).\nContinuation mode is therefore recommended as the canonical condition for cross-model comparisons.\n\n\\paragraph{Neural probe.}\nReplacing hand-crafted features with frozen encoder hidden states (MLP probe; Table~\\ref{tab:s7_neural_probe}), we evaluate $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}}$ across all three model families (Table~\\ref{tab:neural_probe_crossmodel}).\nQwen-continuation achieves 2v3 $\\auc = 0.6159$ $[0.610, 0.622]$, yielding $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}} = 3$.\nHowever, Pythia ($\\auc_{2v3} = 0.5963$ $[0.589, 0.603]$) and OLMo ($\\auc_{2v3} = 0.5892$ $[0.582, 0.597]$) both fall below $\\theta$, giving $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}} = 2$.\nThe Qwen-only elevation likely reflects self-familiarity: each model's frozen embeddings probe its own generations, and Qwen's instruction-tuned representations encode mode-specific artifacts that boost boundary discrimination.\nThe 15D model-free features yield $\\Kempirical = 2$ universally (3/3 models), validating their choice for cross-model deployment.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Neural probe pairwise $\\auc$ with bootstrap 95\\% CIs (frozen-encoder MLP). $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}} = 3$ for Qwen only; Pythia and OLMo match the 15D boundary at $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}} = 2$.}\n\\label{tab:neural_probe_crossmodel}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{2v3 $\\auc$} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen-1.5B  & 0.6159 & $[0.610,\\,0.622]$ & 3 \\\\\nPythia-1.4B & 0.5963 & $[0.589,\\,0.603]$ & 2 \\\\\nOLMo-1B    & 0.5892 & $[0.582,\\,0.597]$ & 2 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n",
        "numLines": 126,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 126
      }
    },

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWeb-scale text corpora are increasingly contaminated by synthetic content that has itself been generated from earlier synthetic content.\nEstimates suggest that a substantial fraction of online text is machine-generated~\\cite{sun2025aigt, spennemann2025quantifying}, and this share continues to rise.\nWhen models train on the outputs of earlier models, the resulting multi-generational synthetic text degrades with each iteration: tail modes vanish, lexical diversity contracts, and surprisal distributions flatten~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024model}.\nYet the dominant paradigm treats all machine-generated text identically, discarding the generational depth dimension.\n\nThe gap cuts across three communities.\nBinary detection methods~\\cite{basani2026diveye, zhang2024minkpp, zawalski2026codec} produce no depth estimate.\nThe model collapse literature~\\cite{shumailov2024collapse, alemohammad2024selfconsuming, schaeffer2025position} characterizes recursive degradation at the macro level but provides no per-text depth estimation tool.\nData selection methods~\\cite{xie2023dsir, tirumala2023d4, drayson2025} ignore recursive depth.\nNo existing method estimates how many rounds of recursive generation a text has undergone, nor quantifies the boundary beyond which such estimation becomes infeasible.\n\nThe Data Processing Inequality guarantees that information degrades monotonically across generations, motivating a finite discrimination boundary $\\Kstar$.\nWe empirically measure $\\Kempirical$---the maximum depth at which a classifier reliably distinguishes adjacent generations---using 15 statistical features and an Ordinal Binary Decomposition (OBD) LightGBM classifier.\nWe operationalize $\\Kempirical$ as the deepest $k$ where the bootstrap 95\\% CI for pairwise $\\auc_{k,k+1}$ exceeds $\\theta = 0.60$.\n\nOur primary contribution is a systematic empirical characterization of $\\Kempirical$:\n\\begin{enumerate}[label=(\\alph*),nosep]\n  \\item \\textbf{$\\Kempirical = 2$} across 4 model-mode configurations and 4 decoding strategies (8 conditions), with all 1v2 CI lower bounds exceeding $\\theta$ and all 2v3 CI upper bounds below $\\theta$ (permutation $p \\leq 0.002$).\n  \\item \\textbf{Cross-model transfer}: 80--92\\% accuracy retention across continuation-mode generators; 0v1 $\\auc > 0.96$ across all pairs.\n  \\item \\textbf{Feature degradation consistency}: Spearman $\\rho \\geq 0.85$ on single-feature rankings across continuation-mode models, with surprisal-variance features dominating.\n  \\item \\textbf{Generation mode}: rewrite yields higher 1v2 $\\auc$ ($+$8.4\\,pp) but transfers poorly (61--63\\% vs.\\ 80--92\\%).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\noindent As a secondary contribution, $\\Kempirical = 2$ correctly predicts that graduated refinement beyond the binary boundary provides no additional benefit: graduated filtering at its best ($\\alpha{=}0.9$, MMLU $0.5892$) still falls short of binary-ours ($0.5941$), and surprisal-based selection ($0.5894$) likewise fails to improve (Section~\\ref{sec:filtering}).\n",
        "numLines": 28,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 28
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/introduction.tex"

    "type": "file",

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/conclusion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe presented the first systematic measurement of the generational depth discrimination boundary $\\Kempirical$ for recursive synthetic text.\nAcross 4 model-mode configurations and 4 decoding strategies (8 conditions total) at the $\\leq$1.5B scale, $\\Kempirical = 2$ is consistent: all 1v2 bootstrap CI lower bounds exceed $\\theta = 0.60$ (permutation $p \\leq 0.002$) while all 2v3 CI upper bounds fall below it.\nCross-model transfer retains 80--92\\% accuracy across continuation-mode generators, with 0v1 $\\auc > 0.96$ across all pairs, indicating that depth signals are largely text-intrinsic.\nNeural probing yields $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}} = 3$ for Qwen-continuation but $\\Kempirical^{\\text{neural}} = 2$ for Pythia and OLMo, confirming $\\Kempirical = 2$ as a representation-independent lower bound; the Qwen elevation reflects model-specific self-familiarity rather than a universal depth signal.\n\nThe $\\Kempirical = 2$ boundary correctly predicts that graduated refinement beyond the binary boundary provides no additional benefit: binary filtering ($0.5941$ MMLU) outperforms graduated depth weighting ($0.5892$ at best $\\alpha$) and surprisal-based selection ($0.5894$), establishing $\\Kempirical$ as an actionable curation guideline.\n\nKey directions for future work include scaling to 7B+ models, extending to multilingual and multi-domain corpora, establishing the prompt-chain vs.\\ retrain-chain $\\Kstar$ relationship, and developing model-agnostic neural probes that overcome the self-familiarity limitation.\n",
        "numLines": 12,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 12
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/conclusion.tex"
  },

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.claude-account-3/projects/-home-ubuntu--agent-ml-research-idea-gen-0509-7-projects-gen-depth-contamination--claude-sessions-reviewer-08f7237f/memory/feedback_pp_unit_conversion.md",
        "content": "---\nname: pp 单位换算必须用 1pp = 0.01\ndescription: 两次犯 10× pp 换算错误（4.7pp→0.47pp, 7.4pp→0.74pp），严格记住 1 percentage point = 0.01 accuracy difference\ntype: feedback\noriginSessionId: 92a9857b-9182-409f-acb8-dd1f4819436d\n---\n报告中涉及 percentage point (pp) 差值时，必须按 1pp = 0.01 换算。\n\n**Why:** 两次在给 Director 的报告中犯了 10× 错误（0.0047 写成 4.7pp 而非 0.47pp；0.0074 写成 7.4pp 而非 0.74pp）。这会严重误导决策——0.74pp 和 7.4pp 是完全不同量级的效果。\n\n**How to apply:** 每次计算 pp 差值时，先算出 raw difference（如 0.5941 - 0.5894 = 0.0047），然后乘以 100 得到 pp（0.0047 × 100 = 0.47pp）。发报告前二次核验所有 pp 数字。\n",
        "numLines": 12,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 12
      }
    },

{
  "parentUuid": "8a824c7b-06be-45ac-8a9b-1b17cca16457",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/experiments_c3.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_7/projects/gen-depth-contamination/docs/paper/experiments_c3.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Application: $\\Kstar$-Aware Data Filtering}\n\\label{sec:filtering}\n\n$\\Kempirical = 2$ correctly predicts that graduated refinement beyond the binary boundary provides no additional benefit over binary filtering.\nWe compare six data curation strategies on a corpus of 30{,}000 documents (5{,}000 per depth, depths 0--5): (i)~\\textbf{no-filter} (uniform weight), (ii)~\\textbf{binary-ours} ($w = 1 - P(\\text{synthetic})$ from the 15D LightGBM classifier), (iii)~\\textbf{binary-Drayson} (importance weighting following \\citet{drayson2025}, same classifier, weights $w_j \\propto (1 - q(x_j))^b$, $b{=}1$, cap $r_{\\max}{=}3$), (iv)~\\textbf{graduated} (exponential decay $w = \\exp(-\\alpha \\cdot \\hat{d})$, $\\alpha \\in \\{0.1,\\ldots,0.9\\}$), (v)~\\textbf{surprisal} (retain documents above corpus median surprisal), and (vi)~\\textbf{DSIR}~\\citep{xie2023dsir} (importance resampling against depth-0 reference).\n\nWe fine-tune Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct via LoRA (rank~8, $\\alpha_{\\text{LoRA}}{=}16$) for 3 epochs, evaluating on MMLU (14{,}042 examples, 0-shot) and HellaSwag (10{,}042 examples, 0-shot) across 5 seeds; the graduated strategy is further crossed with 5~$\\alpha$ values (51 total runs).\n\n\\paragraph{Results.}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Downstream performance of six data filtering strategies. Fine-tuning: Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct, LoRA, 30K documents, 5 seeds. Graduated shows best $\\alpha$ only; full sweep in Table~\\ref{tab:tab_alpha_sweep}.}\n\\label{tab:tab_filtering_main}\n\\input{tables/tab_filtering_main}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll strategies degrade absolute performance relative to the base model (MMLU 0.6018, HellaSwag 0.6821).\nBinary-ours achieves the highest accuracy on both benchmarks (MMLU $0.5941 \\pm 0.0008$; HellaSwag $0.6701 \\pm 0.0006$), outperforming no-filter by $+$0.76\\,pp / $+$0.63\\,pp and binary-Drayson ($0.5913$; $0.6691$) by $+$0.28\\,pp / $+$0.10\\,pp.\nGraduated filtering at its best $\\alpha$ does not outperform binary-ours on either benchmark.\nSurprisal-based selection (MMLU $0.5894 \\pm 0.0009$; HellaSwag $0.6641 \\pm 0.0006$) falls short by $-$0.47\\,pp / $-$0.60\\,pp.\nDSIR performs worst among all strategies (MMLU $0.5869 \\pm 0.0003$; HellaSwag $0.6631 \\pm 0.0007$), only $+$0.04\\,pp above no-filter on MMLU and $-$0.07\\,pp below on HellaSwag---its bigram feature hashing captures domain shift but not the depth-correlated distributional signatures that our classifier exploits.\n\n\\paragraph{$\\alpha$ sensitivity.}\nHigher $\\alpha$ values yield monotonically better performance on both benchmarks (HellaSwag: $0.6640$ at $\\alpha{=}0.1$ to $0.6701$ at $\\alpha{=}0.9$).\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.9$, graduated filtering narrows the MMLU gap to 0.49\\,pp below binary-ours ($0.5892$ vs.\\ $0.5941$) and matches binary-ours exactly on HellaSwag ($0.6701$)---consistent with higher $\\alpha$ progressively approximating binary thresholding.\nThe fact that even the best graduated configuration does not reach binary-ours confirms that ordinal depth assignments for $d \\geq 2$ introduce noise rather than signal (detailed $\\alpha$ sweep in Appendix~\\ref{sec:extended_limitations}).\n\n\\paragraph{Summary.}\n$\\Kempirical = 2$ correctly predicts that graduated refinement provides no additional benefit.\nBinary $\\Kstar$-aware filtering is the most effective strategy among all tested approaches: neither ordinal depth weighting, surprisal scoring, nor distribution-matching (DSIR) improve upon binary curation at the $\\Kempirical$ boundary.\n",
        "numLines": 33,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 33
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/experiments_c3.tex"
  },

```text
I need to compose and post my response to the Director's 6 questions about the rebuttal action matrix. Let me gather the remaining context I need, then synthesize my answers.

Let me recall what I know and what I still need to verify:

Q1: Cohen's h for θ=0.60 — I need to evaluate whether this is convincing
Q2: Pythia as sole alternative scorer — need to assess
Q3: self_BLEU ranking — I have data: not in top-3, ranks #1 only for Greedy
Q4: W1 7B defensibility — need to assess


Q5: 3 citations — all verified real
Q6: Missing issues — need to synthesize

I'm reviewing the Director's original rebuttal action matrix request and checking the paper for specific claims about Cohen's h and scorer independence. I have enough context to work with, so let me verify the Cohen's h calculation and effect size interpretation for AUC values around 0.60 before finalizing my response.

The θ=0.60 threshold used to define K*_empirical maps to a Cohen's h of approximately 0.20 when comparing AUC=0.60 against chance performance at 0.50. This puts it right at the boundary between negligible and small effect sizes, which actually provides reasonable justification for the threshold choice — it represents the minimum detectable effect that's statistically meaningful.

A reviewer might still question why 0.60 specifically rather than 0.55 or 0.65, but Cohen's h gives a principled answer to that concern. The paper already addresses this through sensitivity analysis showing that at θ=0.55 the result shifts to K*=3, while at θ=0.65 it drops to K*=1, demonstrating the threshold isn't arbitrary even if the exact value is somewhat sensitive.

For the Pythia alternative scorer question, the core issue is potential confounding — since 9 of 15 features rely on surprisal from a fixed scorer model, using a scorer from the same model family as the generator could introduce bias rather than genuinely test robustness.

One alternative scorer showing consistent K*=2 would be sufficient to demonstrate the result isn't an artifact of scorer choice, though ideally multiple alternatives would be stronger. Given timeline constraints, one is defensible.

On the 7B model defensibility question, without actually running those experiments, the defense rests on several points: the theoretical framework is scale-independent, the 15D features are computed from text statistics rather than model internals, and the cross-model transfer results suggest the features capture text-intrinsic properties. This is listed as future work and the paper's core contribution is the methodology and boundary concept rather than scale claims, but this argument is admittedly weak without empirical validation at that scale.

The real challenge is that a reviewer demanding "show me 7B" is hard to counter without data. The critical question becomes whether the rebuttal period allows time for even a single 7B experiment — and given this team's capabilities with AI agents and GPU access, running one generation and classification pipeline might actually be feasible.

Looking back at the Director's specific concern about W1, I need to clarify what that warning level item actually covers. The major issue I'm seeing is the exp-015 v3 results showing [2,2,3] — the paper currently presents K*(neural)=3 as universal when it's actually only true for Qwen. This critical finding needs honest acknowledgment in any rebuttal, along with updating the severely outdated paper_framing.md document and addressing the discussion section gaps.

I should verify the scorer-generator distinction in the paper and check the method section to understand the scorer model setup properly.

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01AdCenHhPRMhFubivXkcvTo`</summary>

```text
Excellent! Now I have all the information I need. Let me compile a comprehensive summary of the findings.

## Summary: Methodology and Feature Definitions

Based on my analysis of the paper's methodology section (method.tex) and appendix (appendix.tex), here are the answers to your three questions:

### 1. Scorer Model Identity

**The scorer is Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct** (the same model used as one of the generators for creating the experimental corpus in rewrite mode).

The method.tex states: "All features are computed from token-level surprisal values via an external scorer model (full definitions in Table~ref{tab:s5_features}, Appendix)."

Table S5 (appendix.tex, line 147) clarifies: "Features are grouped into three families: surprisal statistics (9~dims) computed from **Qwen2.5-1.5B token-level log-probabilities**, lexical diversity (3~dims), and frequency tail shape (3~dims)."

### 2. Scorer vs. Generator Relationship

**The scorer (Qwen2.5-1.5B) is THE SAME as one of the generator models, but DIFFERENT from the other generator models tested.**

The paper evaluates four configurations with different generator models:
- Qwen-rewrite: Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct (rewrite mode)
- Qwen-cont: Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct (continuation mode)  
- Pythia: Pythia-1.4B (continuation mode)
- OLMo: OLMo-1B (continuation mode)

However, **all surprisal-based features use a fixed scorer: Qwen2.5-1.5B**. This is a critical design choice: when evaluating Pythia and OLMo outputs, they are scored using Qwen's log-probabilities, not their own. This enables cross-model transfer without retraining, as noted in method.tex line 31: "Hand-crafted features enable cross-generator transfer without retraining."

### 3. Definition of the 15 Features (Especially the 9 Surprisal-Based Ones)

The 15 features are grouped into **four families**:

**A. Surprisal Moments (4 dims) - from token-level log-probabilities:**
1. `surp_mean`: Mean token surprisal = (1/T) * Σ_t [-log p(x_t | x_{<t})]
2. `surp_std`: Standard deviation of token surprisal
3. `surp_skew`: Skewness of the surprisal distribution
4. `surp_kurt`: Excess kurtosis of the surprisal distribution

**B. Surprisal Derivatives (5 dims) - measuring changes in surprisal:**
5. `surp_d1_mean`: Mean of first-order surprisal differences (Δs_t = s_{t+1} - s_t)
6. `surp_d1_std`: Std of first-order surprisal differences
7. `surp_d1_skew`: Skewness of first-order surprisal differences
8. `surp_d2_mean`: Mean of second-order differences (Δ²s_t)
9. `surp_d2_std`: Std of second-order surprisal differences

**C. Vocabulary Diversity (3 dims):**
10. `ttr`: Type-token ratio = |V| / T
11. `hapax_ratio`: Hapax legomena ratio (freq-1 types / unique types)
12. `self_bleu`: Mean 4-gram BLEU against 100 random same-depth samples

**D. Frequency Tail Statistics (3 dims):**
13. `freq_kurtosis`: Excess kurtosis of word frequency distribution
14. `freq_entropy`: Shannon entropy of word frequencies = -Σ p_w log_2(p_w)
15. `low_freq_ratio`: Proportion of vocabulary with count ≤ 2

The key insight is that the 9 surprisal-based features (groups A and B) all derive from token-level log-probabilities computed by the fixed Qwen2.5-1.5B scorer, making them model-independent statistical properties that generalize across different generators.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have all the context I need. Let me compose my response to the Director's 6 questions.

Let me think through each carefully:

**Q1: Cohen's h for θ=0.60 — convincing?**

Cohen's h = 2*arcsin(sqrt(p1)) - 2*arcsin(sqrt(p2))
For AUC=0.60 vs chance=0.50:
h = 2*arcsin(sqrt(0.60)) - 2*arcsin(sqrt(0.50))


= 2*arcsin(0.7746) - 2*arcsin(0.7071)
= 2*0.8861 - 2*0.7854
= 1.7722 - 1.5708
= 0.2014

So h ≈ 0.20, which lands right at Cohen's conventional "small" effect threshold — that's actually a solid justification for why θ=0.60 makes sense. Though I should note that Cohen's h was originally designed for comparing two proportions rather than AUC values, so there's a conceptual mismatch I need to address.

The stronger move is to pair Cohen's h with the empirical validation from the C3 filtering results. The paper already demonstrates that the threshold matters across different θ values (0.55→K*=3, 0.60→K*=2, 0.65→mixed), and the filtering results show that the binary K*=2 boundary actually outperforms the graduated approach. Using both the statistical justification and the task-based evidence together makes a more compelling case than either alone.

Now I'm moving on to the next question about whether Pythia alone could work as an alternative scorer.

The core issue is that Qwen2.5-1.5B acts as both scorer and generator, creating a confound. Using Pythia as an alternative scorer on Qwen-generated text would eliminate that match and test whether K*=2 holds when the scorer and generator are different. That would be sufficient for a rebuttal, though adding OLMo as a second alternative scorer would strengthen the argument further. Since both could be done in roughly an hour total, it's worth considering doing both.

The real subtlety here is that the surprisal features themselves depend on the scorer's log-probabilities, so demonstrating that K*=2 holds with a different scorer would directly address the confound concern. Pythia alone should satisfy reviewers as a sanity check, but OLMo as well would preempt follow-up questions about scorer robustness.

Now looking at the self_BLEU feature importance—checking the paper's results, self_BLEU doesn't appear in the top-3 features for any model-mode combination.  Across Pythia, OLMo, and both Qwen variants, the surprisal derivatives consistently dominate the rankings. Self_BLEU does rank first for greedy decoding specifically, but that's an outlier case rather than representative of the main findings. In the core 15-dimensional feature set, the surprisal family clearly takes precedence.

Removing self_BLEU from the 14D ablation should be relatively safe since it belongs to the smaller vocabulary diversity family, not the dominant surprisal group. For defending the lack of 7B experiments, I can lean on the theoretical argument that DPI is scale-independent, plus the evidence from three different architectures already showing consistent patterns. The features themselves are model-agnostic text statistics rather than architecture-specific internals, and larger models should preserve at least as much information as smaller ones, potentially raising the discrimination boundary rather than lowering it. Combined with an explicit acknowledgment of this limitation and framing it as future work, this might hold up for a borderline paper, though a reviewer who already leaned toward rejection likely sees this gap as more critical.

The real constraint is feasibility. Generating 30K texts across depths 0-5 from a 7B model, extracting 15D features, and training the classifier could realistically take 2-3 days with adequate GPU resources, which fits within the remaining timeline. The bottleneck is whether the available hardware can handle 7B generation—a single A6000 might be tight for this workload.

I should recommend attempting the 7B experiment if GPU memory allows, since it would transform a major weakness into a strength. The fallback of narrowing the title to "≤1.5B scale" is viable but less ideal. I need to verify the actual VRAM available on the server rather than guessing specs.

Now I'm checking the citations—all three sources appear to be legitimate papers.

There's a critical inconsistency in the exp-015 v3 results that needs fixing before rebuttal: the paper treats K*(neural) as uniformly 3, but the actual values vary across models [2, 2, 3]. This discrepancy and the self-familiarity explanation should be explicitly addressed in the rebuttal action matrix.

Wait, let me check the actual paper text more carefully. Looking at the experiments and conclusion sections, the paper already correctly reports the [2, 2, 3] breakdown—Qwen-continuation yields K*(neural) = 3 while both Pythia and OLMo give K*(neural) = 2. The table and conclusion both accurately reflect this variation, so the paper is already being honest about the heterogeneous results. W6's suggestion about rewording the "representation-independent lower bound" language doesn't fully capture this nuance, but the underlying data presentation appears sound.

The W6 wording fix is appropriate. Beyond that, there are some internal document issues—paper_framing.md still reflects an older version and discussion.tex has stale placeholder text about graduated filtering that needs cleanup before submission. More concerning is that the W2 effect sizes are genuinely quite small: the differences between binary-ours and the graduated or surprisal baselines are under half a percentage point, which could lead reviewers to question whether these differences have practical significance despite statistical significance.

The better framing for W2 is to emphasize that K*=2 correctly predicts no benefit from graduated filtering, rather than claiming binary significantly outperforms—the story is about the ordering being right, not the magnitude. I should also increase the permutation resamples from 500 to 10,000 for W8 to get a more precise p-value instead of just reporting the floor.

For C2, there's a critical design issue: using Pythia as a scorer on Qwen data means retraining the classifier on the new feature distributions, since the two models have different scales and architectures. It's a valid experiment, but the Director needs to ensure we're actually retraining the OBD-LightGBM on Pythia-scored features rather than just swapping scorers.

Now for Q4 about the 7B model — the A6000 server has 48GB of memory, which is plenty for a 7B model at fp16 (~14GB). The real bottleneck is generating 30K texts at depth 0-5, which amounts to roughly 3M tokens total. The scoring and classifier training are lightweight by comparison, so the feasibility hinges on generation speed on the A6000.

Working through the sequential generation pipeline: each depth level requires generating from the previous level's outputs, so I'm looking at 5 depths × 5K texts per depth × ~100 tokens each. At an estimated 50 tokens/sec on the A6000, that's about 3 hours per depth, totaling around 15 hours across all depths — very doable within 1-2 days. Running this one 7B experiment would meaningfully strengthen the paper, and I'd recommend it as the primary plan with a fallback option if needed.

Now I'm thinking about what else the Director might be overlooking. The graduated filtering sensitivity actually demonstrates something important: as α increases toward infinity, the behavior converges to binary filtering, and the fact that higher α consistently performs better with α=0.9 nearly matching binary filtering is strong evidence that K*=2. This deserves a more forceful framing in the rebuttal rather than just a defensive posture.

I'm also noticing DSIR's performance is concerning — it barely outperforms no filtering at all, which suggests distribution matching alone doesn't capture depth signals effectively. Though there's a potential counterargument that DSIR's bigram hashing might be too coarse-grained, and a reviewer could push back asking whether better feature representations would help.

Looking at how all strategies degrade...

The base model achieves 0.6018 on MMLU, but even the best fine-tuned approach (binary-ours at 0.5941) falls below that. This reframes the entire problem: we're not trying to improve performance, we're minimizing the damage from fine-tuning on this corpus. That distinction needs to be explicit in the response.

I think I have enough to structure a comprehensive rebuttal now. For the key questions: Cohen's h is partially convincing but should be combined with task-based validation from the C3 results; Pythia alone is sufficient though adding OLMo would strengthen it if time allows; self_BLEU isn't a top-3 metric for any configuration so the 14D ablation is low risk; and the W1 7B limitation can be defended textually but represents a weak point for a borderline paper — though running one additional 7B experiment is feasible.

The citations all check out. The main remaining issues are that the paper framing and discussion sections are outdated, the W2 effect sizes are genuinely small, the permutation resamples need to be increased, and C2 requires careful experimental design to retrain the classifier on the new features.
```

OLMo）> W1（7B 实验）> C3（14D ablation）> W8（增加 resamples）。其中 W1 的 7B 实验虽标 Warning，但对 borderline 论文的命运影响可能最大。",
  "project": "gen-depth-contamination"
}

\\\"surp_d1_mean\\\", \\\"surp_d1_std\\\", \\\"surp_d1_skew\\\",\\n    \\\"surp_d2_mean\\\", \\\"surp_d2_std\\\",\\n]\\nVOCAB_COLS = [\\\"ttr\\\", \\\"hapax_ratio\\\", \\\"self_bleu\\\"]\\nTAIL_COLS = [\\\"freq_kurtosis\\\", \\\"freq_entropy\\\", \\\"low_freq_ratio\\\"]\\nALL_FEAT_COLS = SURPRISAL_COLS + VOCAB_COLS + TAIL_COLS\\n\\nN_SUBSAMPLE = 500\\nN_PER_DEPTH = 100\\nMAX_DEPTH = 4\\nN_FOLDS = 5\\nN_BOOTSTRAP = 1000\\nN_REPEATS = 10\\nSEEDS = list(range(42, 42 + N_REPEATS))\\n\\n\\ndef load_features():\\n    feat_path = DATA_DIR / \\\"features.csv\\\"\\n    with open(feat_path) as f:\\n        reader = csv.DictReader(f)\\n        rows = list(reader)\\n\\n    depths = np.array([int(r[\\\"depth\\\"]) for r in rows])\\n    doc_ids = np.array([int(r[\\\"doc_id\\\"]) for r in rows])\\n    feat_matrix = np.array(\\n        [[float(r[c]) if r[c] not in (\\\"\\\", \\\"nan\\\") else np.nan for c in ALL_FEAT_COLS] for r in rows]\\n    )\\n    # filter to depths 0-4\\n    mask = depths <= MAX_DEPTH\\n    depths = depths[mask]\\n    doc_ids = doc_ids[mask]\\n    feat_matrix = feat_matrix[mask]\\n\\n    # fill NaN with column mean\\n    col_means = np.nanmean(feat_matrix, axis=0)\\n    for j in range(feat_matrix.shape[1]):\\n        nan_mask = np.isnan(feat_matrix[:, j])\\n        feat_matrix[nan_mask, j] = col_means[j]\\n\\n    return depths, doc_ids, feat_matrix\\n\\n\\ndef train_cv_oof(depths, doc_ids, feat_matrix):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"5-fold GroupKFold CV, return OOF predictions.\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    import lightgbm as lgb\\n    from sklearn.model_selection import GroupKFold\\n\\n    gkf = GroupKFold(n_splits=N_FOLDS)\\n    oof_preds = np.full(len(depths), -1, dtype=int)\\n\\n    for train_idx, test_idx in gkf.split(feat_matrix, depths, groups=doc_ids):\\n        clf = lgb.LGBMClassifier(\\n            n_estimators=300, max_depth=8, learning_rate=0.05,\\n            num_leaves=63, verbose=-1, n_jobs=-1,\\n            num_class=MAX_DEPTH + 1,\\n            objective=\\\"multiclass\\\",\\n        )\\n        clf.fit(feat_matrix[train_idx], depths[train_idx])\\n        oof_preds[test_idx] = clf.predict(feat_matrix[test_idx]).astype(int)\\n\\n    return oof_preds\\n\\n\\ndef subsample_accuracy(depths, oof_preds, seed):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Sample 100 per depth, return accuracy on the 500 subset.\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    rng = random.Random(seed)\\n    indices = []\\n    for d in range(MAX_DEPTH + 1):\\n        d_indices = np.where(depths == d)[0].tolist()\\n        chosen = rng.sample(d_indices, N_PER_DEPTH)\\n        indices.extend(chosen)\\n    indices = np.array(indices)\\n    return float(np.mean(oof_preds[indices] == depths[indices]))\\n\\n\\ndef bootstrap_ci(depths, oof_preds, indices, n_bootstrap=N_BOOTSTRAP, seed=0):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Bootstrap CI on a fixed subset.\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    rng = np.random.RandomState(seed)\\n    y_true = depths[indices]\\n    y_pred = oof_preds[indices]\\n    n = len(indices)\\n    accs = []\\n    for _ in range(n_bootstrap):\\n        boot_idx = rng.randint(0, n, size=n)\\n        accs.append(float(np.mean(y_pred[boot_idx] == y_true[boot_idx])))\\n    return np.array(accs)\\n\\n\\ndef main():\\n    print(\\\"Loading features...\\\")\\n    depths, doc_ids, feat_matrix = load_features()\\n    print(f\\\"  {len(depths)} samples, depths 0-{MAX_DEPTH}\\\")\\n\\n    print(\\\"Training 5-fold CV...\\\")\\n    oof_preds = train_cv_oof(depths, doc_ids, feat_matrix)\\n    full_acc = float(np.mean(oof_preds == depths))\\n    print(f\\\"  Full accuracy (N={len(depths)}): {full_acc:.4f}\\\")\\n\\n    print(f\\\"Running {N_REPEATS} subsample evaluations...\\\")\\n    seed_accs = []\\n    all_boot_accs = []\\n    for seed in SEEDS:\\n        acc = subsample_accuracy(depths, oof_preds, seed)\\n        seed_accs.append(acc)\\n\\n        # bootstrap on this subsample\\n        rng = random.Random(seed)\\n        indices = []\\n        for d in range(MAX_DEPTH + 1):\\n            d_indices = np.where(depths == d)[0].tolist()\\n            chosen = rng.sample(d_indices, N_PER_DEPTH)\\n            indices.extend(chosen)\\n        indices = np.array(indices)\\n        boot_accs = bootstrap_ci(depths, oof_preds, indices, seed=seed)\\n        all_boot_accs.extend(boot_accs.tolist())\\n        print(f\\\"  seed={seed}: acc={acc:.4f}, boot_ci=[{np.percentile(boot_accs, 2.5):.4f}, {np.percentile(boot_accs, 97.5):.4f}]\\\")\\n\\n    seed_accs = np.array(seed_accs)\\n    all_boot_accs = np.array(all_boot_accs)\\n\\n    # aggregate across seeds: mean ± std of subsample accuracies\\n    mean_acc = float(np.mean(seed_accs))\\n    std_acc = float(np.std(seed_accs))\\n    # aggregate bootstrap CI: pool all bootstrap samples\\n    ci_lower = float(np.percentile(all_boot_accs, 2.5))\\n    ci_upper = float(np.percentile(all_boot_accs, 97.5))\\n\\n    gpt_acc = 0.332\\n    claude_acc = 0.3307\\n\\n    if mean_acc > max(gpt_acc, claude_acc):\\n        comparison = \\\"above\\\"\\n    elif mean_acc < min(gpt_acc, claude_acc):\\n        comparison = \\\"below\\\"\\n    else:\\n        comparison = \\\"comparable to\\\"\\n\\n    result = {\\n        \\\"obd_n500_accuracy\\\": {\\n            \\\"mean\\\": round(mean_acc, 4),\\n            \\\"std\\\": round(std_acc, 4),\\n            \\\"ci_lower\\\": round(ci_lower, 4),\\n            \\\"ci_upper\\\": round(ci_upper, 4),\\n        },\\n        \\\"obd_full_accuracy\\\": round(full_acc, 4),\\n        \\\"obd_n5000_accuracy_reported\\\": 0.386,\\n        \\\"gpt55_n500_accuracy\\\": gpt_acc,\\n        \\\"claude_n500_accuracy\\\": claude_acc,\\n        \\\"n_bootstrap\\\": N_BOOTSTRAP,\\n        \\\"n_repeats\\\": N_REPEATS,\\n        \\\"seeds\\\": 3. **Surprise**: obd_full_accuracy = 0.4541, but the paper reports 0.386 for OBD (obd_n5000_accuracy_reported). 

I'm noticing the full accuracy (0.4541) doesn't match the paper's reported 0.386, so let me dig into what's causing this mismatch. Looking at the details, the full accuracy comes from training on Pythia data with 5-class depths (0-4) using 5-fold GroupKFold cross-validation, while the reported 0.386 comes from a different experiment that might have used different data or setup.

Checking the script more carefully to understand the discrepancy...

The script claims to implement OBD-LightGBM, but it's actually just using standard multiclass classification with LightGBM, not the ordinal binary decomposition method described in the paper. The classifier is configured with multiclass objective and a single model, whereas true OBD would decompose the ordinal problem into multiple binary classifiers.

Looking at the accuracy discrepancy — exp_010 reported multiclass and OBD accuracies both around 0.38x for Pythia, but exp_D is getting 0.4541. The difference likely comes down to hyperparameter choices (exp_D uses more aggressive settings like 300 estimators and depth 8), the GroupKFold split strategy by document ID, or the depth range being tested (exp_D uses 5 classes instead of whatever was used originally). used depths 0-5 (6 classes). 5-class is easier than 6-class!

Wait, that's the key — exp_D uses MAX_DEPTH=4 (depths 0-4, 5 classes), but the LLM baseline in exp_011 also used 5 depths (0-4), 500 samples. Let me check...

From exp_011: "sampling: 100 texts × 5 depths (0-4) from Pythia continuation" — yes, depths 0-4, 5 classes.

But the paper reports OBD accuracy as 0.386 for 5-class (exp_011 baseline). The discrepancy is between exp_011's OBD baseline (0.386) and exp_D's full accuracy (0.4541).

The difference likely comes down to how the classifiers were set up — exp_011 might have used a random train/test split while exp_D applied GroupKFold with more aggressive hyperparameters. Or exp_011's baseline was trained on the full 6-class problem including depth 5, whereas exp_D focused on depths 0-4. Looking back at the earlier experiments, exp_001_pythia shows ordinal accuracy of 0.383 on 6 classes, and exp_010 reports similar numbers around 0.385-0.386, also likely on 6 classes. So the baseline discrepancy probably stems from whether we're comparing 5-class versus 6-class setups.

This is a critical issue I need to flag — the exp_D results can't be directly compared to exp_011's LLM results without clarifying the exact classifier setup and evaluation methodology.

The script labels itself as "OBD-LightGBM" but actually runs plain multiclass LightGBM, and the full accuracy (0.4541) is substantially higher than the previously reported OBD baseline (0.386) on the same data, suggesting different hyperparameters or evaluation approach. For a fair W9 comparison, I should take the existing OBD classifier trained as described in the paper, evaluate it on the exact same N=500 subset from exp_011, and compare directly rather than retraining with different settings. Let me verify whether the N=500 subset being used matches exp_011's samples. Even with these methodological issues, the core finding still holds — OBD at N=500 substantially outperforms the LLM baseline, so the conclusion is defensible. But I should flag these problems since a reviewer could legitimately critique the comparison as unfair, particularly the inflated OBD accuracy that makes it look artificially strong.
